INTRODUCTION  1 
0.1 General  2 
IEC 61131 -9 is part of a series of standards on programmable controllers and the associated 3 
peripherals and should be read in conjunction with the other parts of the series . 4 
Where a conflict exists between this and other IEC standards (except basic safety standards), 5 
the provisions of this standard should be considered to govern in the area of programmable 6 
controllers and their associated peripherals.  7 
The increased use of micro -controllers embedded in low -cost sensors and actuators has 8 
provided opportunities for adding diagnos is and configuration data to support increasing 9 
application requirements.  10 
The driving force for the SDCI (IO -Link™1)) technology is the need of these low -cost sensors 11 
and actuators to exchange this diagnosis and configuration data with a controller (PC or PLC) 12 
using a low -cost, digital communication technology while maintaining backward compatibility 13 
with the current DI/DO signals.  14 
In fieldbus concepts, the SDCI technology defines a generic interface for connecting sensors 15 
and actuators to a Master unit, which may be combined with gateway capabilities to become a 16 
fieldbus remote I/O node.  17 
Any SDCI compliant Device can be attached to any available interface port  of the Master . SDCI 18 
compliant Devices perform physical to digital conversion in the Device, and then  communicate 19 
the result directly in a standard format using "coded switching" of the 24  V I/O signalling line , 20 
thus removing the need for different DI, DO, AI, AO m odules and a variety of cables.  21 
Physical topology is point -to-point from each Device to the Master using 3 wires over distances 22 
up to 20  m. The SDCI physical interface is backward compatible with the usual 24  V I/O 23 
signalling specified in IEC 61131 -2. Transmission rates of 4,8  kbit/s, 38,4  kbit/s and 24 
230,4  kbit/s are supported.  25 
The Master of the SDCI interface detects, identifies and manages Devices plugged into its ports.  26 
Tools allow the association of Devices with their corresponding electronic I/O Device Des-27 
criptions (IODD) and their subsequent configuration to match the application requirements.  28 
The SDCI technology specifies three different levels of diagnostic capabilities: for immediate 29 
response by automated needs during the production phase, for medium term response by 30 
operator intervention, or for longer term commissioning and maintenance via extended 31 
diagnosis information.  32 
The structure of this standard  is described in 4.8. 33 
Conformity  with IEC 61131 -9 cannot be claimed unless the requirements of Annex  H are met.  34 
Terms of general use are defined in IEC 61131 -1 or in the IEC 60050  series . More specific 35 
terms are defined in each part.  36

that compliance with this document may involve the use of patents concerning the point -to-point 39 
serial communication interface for small sensors and actuators as follows, where the [xx] 40 
notation indicates the holder of the patent right:  41

PROGRAMMABLE CONTROLLERS — 55 
 56 
Part 9: Single -drop digital  communication interface  57 
for small sensors and actuators  (SDCI)  58 
 59 
 60 
1 Scope  61 
This part of IEC 61131 specifies a  single -drop digital communication interface technology for 62 
small sensors and actuators SDCI (commonly known as IO -Link ™2), which  extends the 63 
traditional  digital input and digital output interfaces as defined in IEC 61131 -2 towards a point -64 
to-point communication link for the exchange of complex data in both directions. This 65 
technology also enables the transfer of parameters to or from Devices and the delivery of 66 
identification and diagnostic information from the Devices to the automation system.  67 
This technology is mainly intended for use with simple sensors and actuators in factory 68 
automation, which include small and cost -effective microcontrollers.   69 
This part  specifie s the SDCI communication  services and protocol ( physical layer, data link 70 
layer and application layer  in accordance  with the ISO/OSI reference model ) for both SDCI 71 
Masters and Devices . 72 
This part also includes EMC test requirements.  73 
This part does not cover communication interfaces or systems incorporating multiple point or 74 
multiple drop linkages, or integration of SDCI into higher level systems such as fieldbuses.  75 
2 Normative references  76

are indispensable for its application. For dated references, only the edition cited applies. For 78 
undated references, the latest edition of the referenced document (including any amendments) 79 
applies.  80

3.3 Conventions  294 
3.3.1  General  295 
The service model, service primitives, and the diagrams shown in this standard  are entirely 296 
abstract descriptions. The implementation of the services may reflect individual issues and can 297 
be different . 298 
3.3.2  Service parameters  299 
Service primitives are used to represent service provider/consumer interactions 300 
(ISO/IEC  10731). They convey parameters which indicate the information available in the 301 
provider/ consumer  interaction. In any particular interface, not each and every parameter needs 302 
to be explicitly stated.  303 
The service specification in this standard  uses a tabular format to describe the component 304 
parameters of the service primitives. The parameters which apply to each group of service 305 
primitives are set out in tables. Each table consists of up to five columns : 306 
1) parameter name ; 307 
2) request primitive  (.req) ; 308 
3) indication primitive  (.ind) ; 309 
4) response primitive  (.rsp) ; and  310 
5) confirm ation  primitive  (.cnf) . 311 
One parameter (or component of it) is listed in each row of each table. Under the appropriate 312 
service primitive columns, a code is used to specify the type of usage of the parameter on the 313 
primitive specified in the column.  314 
M Parameter is mandatory for the primitive.  315 
U Parameter is a user option  and can or cannot be provided depending on dynamic usage 316 
of the service user. When  not provided  a default value for the parameter is assumed.  317 
C Parameter is conditional upon other parameters or upon the environment of the service 318 
user.  319 
– Parameter is never present.  320 
S Parameter is a selected item.  321 
 322 
Some entries are further qualified by items in brackets. These may be : 323 
a) a parameter -specific constraint  "(=)" indicates that the parameter is semantically equiva -324 
lent to the parameter in the service primitive to its immediate left in the table;  325 
b) an indication that some note applies to the entry  "(n)" indicates that the following note "n" 326 
contains additional information related  to the parameter and its use.  327 
3.3.3  Service procedures  328 
The procedures are defined in terms of : 329 
• the interactions between application entities through the exchange of protocol data units ; 330 
and 331 
• the interactions between a communication layer service provider and a communication layer 332 
service consumer  in the same system through the invocation of service primitives.  333 

===PAGE 41===
Interface and System  © IO -Link  – 41 – V1.1.5  
These procedures are applicable to instances of communication between systems which 334 
support time -constrained communications services within the communication layers.  335 
3.3.4  Service attributes  336 
The nature of the different (Master and Device) services is characterized by attributes. All 337 
services are defined from the view of the affected layer towards the layer above.  338 
I Initiator of a service  (towards the layer above)  339 
R Receiver (r esponder) of a service  (from the layer above)  340 
3.3.5  Figures  341 
For figures that show the structure and services of protocol layers, the following conventions 342 
are used:  343 
• an arrow with just a service name represents both a request and the corresponding 344 
confirmation, with the request being in the direction of the arrow;  345 
• a request without confirmation, as well as all indications and responses are labelled as such 346 
(i.e. service.req, service.ind, service.rsp).  347 
Figure 1 shows the example of a confirmed service.  348

Figure 1 – Example  of a confirmed service  350

3.3.7  Behavioral descriptions  357 
For the behavioral descriptions, the notations of UML 2 (ISO/IEC  19505)  are used  (e.g. state , 358 
sequence, activity,  timing  diagrams , guard conditions) .  359 
State diagrams are the primary source for implementations whereas sequence charts illustrate 360 
certain use cases.  361 
Characteristics of s tate diagrams are   362 
• triggers/events  coming from external requests ("calls") or internal changes such as timeouts;  363 
• [guard (s)] as Boolean expressions  for exits of states;  364 
• numbered transitions describing  actions in addition to the triggers within separate state -365 
transition tables.  366 
The layout of these tables is following IEC/TR  62390.  367 
In this document, the concept of "nested states" with superstates and substates is used as 368 
shown in the example of Figure 3. 369 
 370 
Figure 3 – Example of a nested state  371 
UML 2 allows hierarchies of states with superstates and substates. The highest superstate 372 
represents the entire state machine.  373 
This concept allows for simplified modelling since the content of superstates can be moved to 374 
a separate drawing. An eyeglasses icon usually represents this content.  375 
Compared to "flat" state machines, a particular set of rules shall be observed for "nested states":  376

a) A transition to the edge of a superstate (e.g. Default_entry) implies transition to the initial 377 
substate (e.g. A_1).  378 
b) Transition to a termination state inside a superstate implies a transition without event and 379 
guard to a state outside (e.g.X_4). The superstate will become inactive.  380 
c) A transition from any of the substates (e.g. A_1, B_2, or C_3) to a state outside (Y_5) can 381 
take place whenever Event1 occurs and Guard1 is true. This is helpful in case of common 382 
errors within the substates. The superstate will become inactive.  383 
d) A transition from a particular substate (e.g. C_3) to a state outside (Z_6) can take place 384 
whenever Event2 occurs and Guard2 is true. The superstate will become inactive.  385 
Due to UML design tool restrictions the following exceptions apply.  386 
For state diagrams, a service parameter (in capital letters) is attached to the service name via 387 
an underscore character, such as for example in DL_SetMode_INACTIVE.  388 
For sequence diagrams, the service primitive is attached via an underscore character instead 389 
of a dot, and the service parameter is added in parenthesis, such as for example in 390 
DL_Event_ind  (OPERATE).   391 
Timing constraints  are labelled "tm(time in ms)".  392 
Asynchronously received service calls are not modelled in detail with in state diagrams.  393 
4 Overview of SDCI (IO -Link TM4) 394 
4.1 Purpose of technology  395 
Figure 4 shows  the basic concept of SDCI.  396 
 397 
Figure 4 – SDCI compatibility with IEC 61131 -2 398 
The single -drop digital communication interface technology for small sensors and actuators 399 
SDCI (commonly known as IO -Link TM) defines a migration path from the existing digital input 400 
and digital output interfaces for switching 24  V Devices as defined in IEC 61131 -2 towards a 401 
point -to-point communication link. Thus, for example, digital I/O modules in existing fieldbus 402 
peripherals can be replaced by SDCI Master modules providing both cla ssic DI/DO interfaces 403 
and SDCI. Analog transmission technology can be replaced by SDCI combining its robust ness, 404 
parameterization, and diagnostic features with the saving of digital/analog and analog/digital 405 
conversion efforts.  406 
  407 
————————— 
4  IO-Link TM is a trade name of the "IO -Link Community". This information is given for the convenience of users of 
this international Standard and does not constitute an endorsement by IEC of the trade name holder or any of its 
products. Compliance to this standard do es not require use of the registered logos for IO -Link TM. Use of the 
registered logos for IO -Link TM requires permission of the "IO -Link Community".

4.2 Positioning within the automation hierarchy  408 
Figure 5 shows  the domain of the SDCI technology within the automation hierarchy.  409 
 410 
Figure 5 – Domain  of the SDCI technology within the automation  hierarchy  411 
The SDCI technology defines a generic interface for connecting sensors and actuators to a 412 
Master unit, which may be combined with gateway capabilities to become a fieldbus remote I/O 413 
node.  414 
Starting point for the design  of SDCI  is the classic 24 V digital input  (DI)  defined in IEC 61131 -415 
2 and output interface (DO)  specified  in Table 6. Thus, SDCI offers connectivity of classic 24  V 416 
sensors ("switching signals") as a default operational mode . Additional connec tivity is provided 417 
for actuators when a port has been configured into "single -drop communication mode".  418 
Many sensors and actuators nowadays are  already equipped with microcontrollers offering a 419 
UART interface that  can be extended by addition  of a few hardware components and protocol 420 
software  to support SDCI communication. This second operational mode uses "coded 421 
switching" of the 24  V I/O signalling line . Once activated, the SDCI mode supports  422 
parameterization, cyclic data exchange, diagnosis reporting , identification & maintenance 423 
information, and external parameter stora ge for Device backup and fast reload of replacement 424 
devices . Sensors and actuators with SDCI  capabilit y are referred to as  "Device s" in this 425 
standard . To improve  start -up performance t hese Device s usually provide non -volatile storage  426 
for parameters . 427 
NOTE  Configuration and parameterization of Device s is supported through  an XML -based device description (see 428 
[6] ), which is not  part of this standard . 429 
4.3 Wiring, connectors  and power  430 
The default connection (port class A) comprises 4 pins  (see  Figure 4). The default wiring for 431 
port class A complies with IEC 60947 -5-2 and uses only three wires for 24  V, 0  V, and a signal 432 
line. The fourth wire may be used as an additional signal line complying with IEC 61131-2. 433 
Five pins connections (port class B) are specified for Devices requiring additional power from 434 
an independant 24  V power supply.  435 
NOTE  A port class A Device using the fourth wire is not compatible  with a port class B Master . 436 
Maximum length of cables is 20  m, shielding is not required.  437

4.4 Communication f eatures of SDCI  438 
The gener ic Device  model is shown in Figure 6 and explained in the following paragraphs . 439 
 440 
Figure 6 – Generic  Device  model for SDCI  (Master 's view)  441 
A Device  may receive Process Data  (out) to control a discrete or continuous automation process 442 
or send Process Data  (in) representing its current state or measurement values. The Device  443 
usually provides parameters enabling the user to configure  its functions to satisfy particular 444 
needs. To support  this case a large  parameter space is defined with access  via an Index  445 
(0 to 65535 ; with a predefined organization) and a Subindex  (0 to 255).  446 
The first two index entries 0 and 1 are  reserved for the Direct Parameter page  1 and 2  with a 447 
maximum of 16 octets  each . Parameter page 1  is mainly dedicated to Master  commands  such 448 
as Device  start up and  fallback, retrieval of Device  specific operational and identification 449 
information. Parameter page 2 allows for a maximum of 1 6 octets of Device  specific parameters . 450 
The other indices ( 2 to 65 535) each allow access to one  record having a maximum size of 232 451 
octets. Subindex 0 specifies transmission of the complete record addressed by the Index, other 452 
subindices specify transfer of selected data items within the  record.  453 
Within a record , individual data item s may start on any bit  offset , and their  length may range 454 
from 1 bit to 232 octets, but the total number of data items in the  record cannot exceed 255.  455 
The organization of data items within a record is specified in the IO Device Description (IODD).  456 
All changes of Device condition that require reporting or intervention are stored within an Event 457 
memory  before transmission. An Event flag is then set in the cyclic data exchange to indicate 458 
the existence of an Event.  459 
Communication between a Master and a Device is point -to-point and is based on the principle 460 
of a Master  first sending a request message and then a Device  sending a response message  461 
(see Figure 38). Both messages together are called a n M-sequence . Several M-sequence  types 462 
are defined to support user requirements for data transmission  (see Figure 39).  463 
Data of various categories are transmitted through separate communication channels within the 464 
data link layer, as shown in Figure 7. 465

 466 
Figure 7 – Relationship between nature of data and transmission types  467 
• Operational data such as Device inputs and outputs is transmitted through a process 468 
channel using cyclic transfer. Operational data may also be associated with qualifiers such 469 
as valid/invalid.  470 
• Configuration and maintenance parameters are transmitted using acyclic transfers. A page 471 
channel is provided for direct access to parameter pages 1 and 2, and an ISDU channel is 472 
used for accessing additional parameters and commands.  473 
• Device events are transmitted using acyclic transfers through a diagnostic channel. Device 474 
events are reported using 3 severity levels, error, warning, and notification.  475 
The first octet of a Master message  controls the data transfer direction (read/write) and the type 476 
of communication  channel.  477 
Figure 8 shows each port of a Master has its own data link layer which interfaces to a common 478 
master application layer. Within the application layer , the services of the data link layer are 479 
translated into actions on Process Data  objects  (input/output) , On-request Data  objects  480 
(read/write) , and event s. Master  application s include  a Configuration Manager (CM), Data 481 
Storage mechanism (DS), Diagnosis Unit (DU), On -request Data E xchange (ODE), and a 482 
Process Data Exchange  (PDE). 483 
System Management  checks identification of the connected Devices and adjusts ports and 484 
Devices to match the chosen configuration and the properties of the connected Devices.  It 485 
controls the state machines in the application (AL) and data link layers (DL) , for example at 486 
start -up. 487

4.5 Role of a Master  490 
A Master  accommodates 1  to n ports and the ir associated data link layers. During start -up it 491 
changes the ports to  the user -selected  port modes, which can be DEACTIVATED , 492 
IOL_MANUAL, IOL_AUTOSTART, DI_C/Q, or DO_C/Q . If communication is requested , the 493 
Master uses a special wake -up current pulse  to initiate communication with the Device . The 494 
Master then  auto -adjust s the transmission rate  to COM1, COM2, or COM3 ( see Table 9) and 495 
checks the "personality" of the connected Device , i.e. its Vendor ID, Device ID, and 496 
communication properties .  497 
If there is a mismatch between the Device  parameters and the stored parameter set within the 498 
Master , the parameters in the Device  are overwritten ( see 11.4 ) or the stored parameters within 499 
the master are updated depending on the configuration . 500 
The Master is responsible for the assembling and disassembling of all data from or to the 501 
Devices  (see Clause  11). 502 
The Master provides a Data Storage  area of at least 2  048 octets per Device for backup  of 503 
Device data (see 11.4 ). The Master  may combine this Device data together with all other 504 
relevant data for its own operation, and make this data  available for higher level applications 505 
for Master backup purpose  or recipe control (see 13.4.2 ). 506 
4.6 SDCI  configuration  507 
Engineering support for a Master  is usually provided by a P ort and Device Configuration T ool 508 
(PDCT). The P DCT configures  both port properties and Device properties (see parameters  509 
shown in Figure 6). It  combin es both an interpreter of the I/O Device D escription (IODD) and a 510 
configurator  (see 13). The IODD  provides all the necessary properties to establish 511 
communication and the necessary parameters and their boundaries to establish the desired 512 
function of a sensor or actuator. The PDCT also supports the compilation of the Process Data  513 
for propagation on the fieldbus and vice versa . 514

4.7 Mapping to fieldbuses  and/or other upper  level systems  515 
Specifications for integration of Masters into upper level  systems such as a fieldbus system, 516 
i.e. the definition of gateway functions for exchanging data with upper level  entities, is out of 517 
scope of this standard. However, all functions of this standard are mandatory to be made 518 
available to the users by a particular integration according to the capability level of the upper 519 
level  system technology except for those functions that are declared explicitly as optional.  520 
EXAMPLE  These functions include mapping of the Process Data exchange, realization of program -controlled 521 
parameterization or a remote parameter server, or the propagation of diagnosis information.  522 
The integration of a PDCT into engineering tools of a particular fieldbus or other upper level  523 
system is out of scope of this standard . 524 
4.8 Standard  structure  525 
Figure 9 shows  the logical structure of the Master  and Device . Clause  5 specifies  the Physica l 526 
Layer  (PL) of SDCI, Clause  6 specifies  details of the SIO mode. Clause  7 specifies  Data Link 527 
Layer (DL) services, protocol, wake -up, M-sequence s, and the DL layer handler s. Clause  0 528 
specifies  the services and the protocol of the Application Layer  (AL)  and clause 9 the System 529 
Management  responsibilities (SM).  530 
 531 
Figure 9 – Logical s tructure of Master and Device  532 
Clause  10 specifies Device applications and features. These include Process Data Exchange 533 
(PDE), Parameter Management (PM), Data Storage (DS), and Event Dispatcher (ED) . 534 
Technology specific Device applications  are not part of this standard . They may be  specified  in 535 
profiles for particular Device  families.  536 
Clause  11 specifies  Master applications and features. These include Process Data Exchange 537 
(PDE), On -request Data Exchange (ODE), Configuration Management (CM), Data Storage (DS)  538 
and Diagnosis Unit (DU) . A Standardized Master Interface (SMI) ensures uniform behavior via 539 
specified services and allows for usage of one PDCT (Master tool ) for different Master brands.  540 
Clause 12 provides a holistic best practice view on Data Storage behavior of both Master and 541 
Device.  542 
Clause 13 outlines integration aspects of IO -Link into various automation and IT realms.   543 
Several normative and informative annexes are included. Annex  A defines the available M-544 
sequence  types. Annex  B describes the parameters of the Direct Parameter page and the fixed 545 
Device parameters. Annex  C lists the error types in case of acyclic transmissions and Annex  D 546 
the EventC odes (diagnosis information of Devices).  Annex  E specifies the coding of argument 547 
blocks for the SMI services.  Annex  F specifies the available basic and composite data types. 548 
Annex  G defines the structure of Data Storage objects. Annex  H deals with conformity and 549 
electromagnetic compatibility test requirements and Annex  I provides graphs of residual error 550 
probabilities, demonstrating the level of SDCI's data integrity. The informative Annex  J provides 551 
an example of the sequence of acyclic data transmissions.  The informative Annex  K explains 552 
two recommended methods for detecting parameter changes in the context of Data Storage.  553

5 Physical Layer (PL)  554 
5.1 General  555 
5.1.1  Basics  556 
The 3 -wire connection system of SDCI is based on the specifications in IEC 60947 -5-2. The 557 
three lines are used as follows: (L+) for the 24  V power supply, (L -) for the ground line, and 558 
(C/Q) for the switching signal (Q) or SDCI communication (C), as shown in  Figure 10.  559 
 560 
Figure 10 – Three wire connection system  561 
NOTE  Binary sensors compliant with IEC 60947 -5-2 are compatible with the SDCI 3 -wire connection system  562 
(including from a power consumption point of view).  563 
Support of the SDCI 3 -wire connection system  is mandatory for Master . Ports with this 564 
characteri stic are called port class A.  565 
Port class A uses a four -pin connector. The fourth wire may be used as an additional signal line 566 
complying with IEC 61131 -2. Its support is optional in both Masters  and Devices.  567 
Five wire connections (port class B) are specified for Devices requiring additional power from 568 
an independant 24  V power supply (see 5.5.1 ). 569 
NOTE  A port class A Device using the fourth wire is not compatible with a port class B Master.  570 
5.1.2  Topology  571 
The SDCI system topology uses  point -to-point links between a Master  and its Device s as shown 572 
in Figure 11. The Master  may have multiple ports for the connection of Device s. Only one Device  573 
shall  be connected to each port.  574 
 575 
Figure 11 – Topology of SDCI  576

5.2 Physical layer services  578 
5.2.1  Overview  579 
Figure 12 shows  an overview of the Master 's physical layer and its service  primitive s. 580 
 581 
Figure 12 – Physical layer ( Master ) 582 
The physical layer specifies the operation of  the C/Q line in Figure 4 and the associated line 583 
driver (transmitter) and receiver of a particular port. The Master  operate s this line in three main  584 
modes  (see Figure 12): inactive, "Switching signal" (DI/DO), or "Coded switching" (COMx ). The 585 
service PL -SetMode.req is responsible for switching into one of these modes.  586 
If the port is in inactive mode, the C/Q line shall be  high impedance (floating). In SIO mode, the 587 
port can be used as a standard input or output interface according to the definitions of 588 
IEC 61131 -2 or in Table 6 respectively . The communication layers of SDCI are bypassed as 589 
shown in Figure 12; the signals are directly processed within the Master  application. In SDCI 590 
mode, the service P L_WakeUp.req creates a special signal pattern (current  pulse ) that can be 591 
detected by an SDCI enabled Device  connected to this port ( see 5.3.3.3 ). 592 
Figure 13 shows  an overview of the Device 's physical layer and its service  primitive s. 593 
The physical layer of a Device according to Figure 13 follows the same principle, except that 594 
there is no inactive state. By default,  at power on or cable reconnection, the Device shall 595 
operate in the SIO mode, as a digital input (from a Master's point of view). The Device shall 596 
always be able to detect a wake up except during a permanent inactive state . The service 597 
PL_WakeUp.ind reports successful detection of the wake -up request (usually a microcontroller 598 
interrupt), which is required for the Device to switch to the SDCI mode.  599 
A special MasterCommand (fallback) sent via SDCI causes the Device to switch back to SIO 600 
mode.  601 
 602 
Figure 13 – Physical layer (Device)  603 
Subsequently, the services are specified  that are provided by the PL to System Management 604 
and to the Data Link Layer (see  Figure 85 and Figure 96 for a complete overview of all the 605 
services) . Table 1 lists the assignments of Master  and Device to their roles as initiator or 606 
receiver  for the individual PL services.  607

5.3 Transmitter/Receiver  656 
5.3.1  Description method  657 
The physical layer is specified by means of electrical and timing requirements . Electrical 658 
requirements  specify signal levels and currents separately for Master  and Device  in the  form of 659 
reference schematics.  Timing requirements  specify the signal transmission process (specifically 660 
the receiver ) and a special signal detection  function . 661 
5.3.2  Electrical requirements  662 
5.3.2.1  General  663 
The line driver is specified  by a reference schematic corresponding to Figure 14. On the Master  664 
side, a transmitter comprises a combination of two line  drivers and one current sink. On the 665 
Device  side, in its simplest form, the transmitter takes the form of a p -switching driver. As an 666 
option there can be an additional n -switching or non -switching driver (this also allows  the option 667 
of push -pull output operation).  668 
In operating status ON the descriptive variables are the residual voltage VRQ, the standard 669 
driver current IQ, and the peak current IQPK . The source is controlled by the On/Off signal. An 670 
over load current event is indicated at the “Overload” output  (OVD ). This feature can be used 671 
for the current pulse detection (wake -up). 672 
 673

The receiver is specified  by a reference schematic according to Figure 15. It performs the 675 
function of a comparator and is specified  by its switching thresholds VTH and a hysteresis VHYS  676 
between the switching thresholds. The output indicates the logic level (High or Low) at the 677 
receiver input.  678

5.3.3  Timing requirements  725 
5.3.3.1  Transmission method  726 
The “ Non Return  to Zero” (NRZ) modulation is used for the bit -by-bit coding. A logic value “1” 727 
corresponds to a voltage difference of 0  V between the C/Q line and L - line. A logic value "0" 728 
corresponds to a voltage difference of +24  V between the C/Q line and L - line.  729 
The open -circuit level on the C/Q line is 0 V with reference to L -. A start bit has logic value “0”, 730 
i.e. +24 V with reference to L -. 731 
A UART frame is used for the "data  octet" -by-"data  octet"  coding. The format of the SDCI UART 732 
frame is a bit string structured as shown in Figure 21. 733 
 734 
Figure 21 – Format of an SDCI UART frame  735

5.3.3.2  Transmission  characteristics  737 
The timing  characteristics of transmission  are demon strated in the form of an eye diagram with 738 
the permissible signal ranges  (see Figure 22). These ranges are applicable for receiver in both 739 
the Master  and the Device .  740 
Regardless of boundary conditions, the transmitter shall generate a voltage characteristic on 741 
the receiver's C/Q connection that is within the permissible range of the eye diagram.  742 
The receiver shall detect bits as a valid signal shape within the permissible range of the eye 743 
diagram on the C/Q connection. Signal shapes in the “no detection” areas  (below VTHL MAX or 744 
above VTHH MIN and within tND) shall not lead to invalid bits.  745

5.3.3.3  Wake -up current pulse  769 
The wake -up feature is used to request that a Device goes to the COMx mode.  770 
A service call (PL_WakeUp.req) from the DL initiates the wak e-up process (see  5.2.2.2 ). 771 
The wake -up request (WURQ) starts with  a current pulse induced by the Master  (port)  for a time 772 
TWU. The wake -up request comprises the following phases  (see Figure 24): 773 
a) Injection of a current IQWU by the Master depending on the level of the C/Q connection. For 774 
an input signal equivalent to logic “1” this is a current source; for an input signal equivalent 775 
to logic “0” this is a current sink.  776 
b) Delay time of the Device until it is ready to receive.  777 
The wake -up request pulse can be detected by the Device through a voltage change on the C/Q 778 
line or evaluation of the current of the respective driver element  within the time TWU. Figure 24 779 
shows examples for Devices with low output power.  780 
 781 
Figure 24 – Wake -up request  782 
Table 10 specifies the current and timing properties  associated with the wake -up request. See 783 
Table 6 for values of IQPKL M and IQPKH M. 784

5.4 Power supply  787 
5.4.1  Power supply options  788 
The SDCI connection system provides dedicated power lines in addition to the signal line. The 789 
communication section of a Device shall always be powered by the Master using the power 790 
lines defined in the 3 -wire connection system  (Power 1 ). 791

Manufacturers/vendors shall emphasize this requirement within the user manual of the Master. 792 
Any additional measure for further increased robustness is within the responsibility of the 793 
designer/manufacturer of the Master.  794 
The m inimum supply current available from a Master port is specified  in Table 6. 795 
The application section  of the Device may be powered in one of three ways:  796 
• via the power lines of the SDCI 3 -wire connection system ( class A ports) , using Power  1 797 
• via the extra power lines of the SDCI 5 -wire connection system ( class B ports) , using an 798 
extra  power supply  at the Master (Power  2) that shall be nonreactive, that means no impact 799 
on voltages and currents of Power  1 and on SDCI communication s 800 
• via a local power supply  at the Device (design  specific ) that shall be nonreactive to Power  1, 801 
thus guaranteeing correct communication even in case of failing local power supply  802 
It is recommended for Devices not to consume more than the minimum current a Master shall 803 
support (see  Table 6). This ensures easiest handling of Master/Device systems without 804 
inquiries, checking, and calculations. Whenever a Device requires more than the minimum 805 
current the capabilities of the respective Master port and of its cabling shall be checked.  806 
5.4.2  Port Class B  807 
Figure 25 shows the layout of the two port classes A and B. Class B ports shall be marked to 808 
distinguish from Class A ports due to risks deriving from incompatibilities on pin 2 and pin 5.  809 
Power 2 on port class B shall meet the following requirements  810 
• electrical isolation of Power 2 from Power  1; 811 
• degree of isolation according to IEC 60664 (clearance and creepage distances);  812 
• electrical safety (SELV) according to IEC 61010 -2-201:2017;  813 
• direct current with P24 (+) and N24 ( -); 814 
• Device shall continue communicating correctl y even in case of failing Power  2. 815 
NOTE: EMC tests should consider maximum ripple and load switching   816 
 817 
A Device designer shall ensure that Power 1 and Power 2 are always electrically isolated even 818 
in particular deployments/applications at the customer's site. Violation of this rule at one port 819 
can have impact on all other ports.    820 
 821 
Figure 25 – Class A and B port definitions  822

side 18 V shall be available in this case.  
b) Minimum current in order to guarantee a high degree of interoperability.  
c) The recommended maximum current for a wire gauge of 0,34 mm 2 and standard M12 connector is 3,5 A. 
Maximum current depends on the type of connector, the wire gauge, maximum temperature, and simultaneity 
factor of the ports (check user manual of a Master).   
 825 
In general, the requirements of Devices shall be checked whether they meet the available 826 
capabilities of the Master. In case a simultaneity factor for Master ports exists, it shall be 827 
documented in the user manual and be observed by the user of the Master.  828 
5.4.3  Power -on requirements  829 
The power -on requirements are specified in 5.3.2.3  and 5.3.2.4 . 830 
5.5 Medium  831 
5.5.1  Connector s 832 
The Master  and Device  pin assignment is based on the specifications in IEC 60947 -5-2, with 833 
extensions specified in the paragraphs below.   834 
Ports class  A use M5, M8, and M12 connectors, with a maximum of five  pins.  835 
Port s class B only use  M12 connectors with 5 pins.   836 
M12 connectors are mechanically A -coded according to IEC 61076 -2-101.  837 
NOTE  For legacy or compatibility reasons, direct wiring or different types of connectors can be used instead, 838 
provided that they  do not violate the electrical characteristics  and use signal naming  specified in this standard . 839 
Female  connectors are assigned to the Master . Table 12 lists the pin assignment s and  840 
Figure 26 shows  the layout  and mechanical coding for M12, M8 , and M5 connections.   841

6 Standard Input and Output (SIO)  867 
Figure 85 and Figure 96 demonstrate  how the SIO mode allows a Device  to bypass the SDCI 868 
communication layers and to map the DI or DO signal directly into the data exchange mes sage 869 
of the upper level  fieldbus or system. Changing between the SDCI and SIO mode is defined by 870 
the user configuration or implicitly  by the services of the Master  appli cations. The System 871 
Management  takes care of the corresponding initialization or deactivation of the SDCI 872 
communication layers and the physical layer (m ode switch). The characteristics of the interfaces 873 
for the DI and DO signals are derived  from  the caracteristics specified in IEC 61131 -2 for type 874 
1. 875 
7 Data link layer  (DL)  876 
7.1 General  877 
The data link layers of SDCI are concerned with the delivery of message s between a Master  878 
and a Device  across the physical link. It uses several M-sequence  ("message  sequence")  types 879 
for different data categories .  880 
A set of DL -services is available  to the application layer (AL) for the exchange of Process Data  881 
(PD)  and On -request Data  (OD) . Another set of DL -services is available to System Management  882 
(SM) for the retrieval of Device communication and identification  parameters and the setting of 883 
state machines within the DL. The DL uses PL-Services for controlling  the physical layer  (PL)  884 
and for exchanging UART frames . The DL takes care of the error detection of messages 885 
(whether internal or repor ted from  the PL) and the appropriate remedial measures  (e.g. retry).  886 
L+
L-
C/QRLeff
CLeff
L

===PAGE 65===
Interface and System  © IO -Link  – 65 – V1.1.5  
The data link layers are structured due to the nature of the data categories into Process Data 887 
handlers and On -request Data handlers which are in turn using a message handler to deal with 888 
the requested transmission of messages. The special modes of Master ports such as wake -up, 889 
COMx, and SIO (disable communication) require a dedicated DL -mode handler within the 890 
Master DL. The special wake -up signal modulation requires signal detection on the Device side 891 
and thus a DL -mode handler within the Device DL. Each handler comprises its own state 892 
machine.   893 
The data link layer is subdivided in a DL -A section with its own internal services and a DL -B 894 
section with the external services. The DL uses additional internal administrative calls between 895 
the handlers which are defined in the "internal items" section of the associated state -transition 896 
tables.  Figure 28 shows an overview of  the structure and the services of the Master 's data link 897 
layer.  898

NOTE  This figure uses the conventions in 3.3.5 . 900

7.2.2  DL-A services  1243 
7.2.2.1  Overview  1244 
According to 7.1 the data link layer is split into the upper layer DL -B and the lower layer DL -A. 1245 
The layer DL -A comprises the message  handler as shown in Figure 28 and Figure 29.  1246 
The Master message  handler encodes commands and data into messages and sends these to 1247 
the connected Device  via the physical layer. It receives messages from the Device  via the 1248 
physical layer and forwards their content to the corresponding handlers in the form of a 1249 
confirmation. When the "Event  flag" is set  in a Device message ( see A.1.5 ), the Master message  1250 
handler invokes an EventFlag service to prompt  the Event  handler .  1251 
The Master  message  handler shall employ  a retry strategy following  a corrupted message , i.e. 1252 
upon receiving an incorrect checksum from a Device , or no checksum at all. In these cases,  the 1253 
Master  shall repeat the Master  message  two times  (see Table 102). If the retries are not 1254 
successful, a negative confirmation shall be provided,  and the Master  shall re -initiate the 1255 
communication via the Port -x handler beginning with a wake-up.  1256 
After a start -up phase t he message  handler performs cyclic operation with the M-sequence  type 1257 
and cycle time provided by the DL_ SetMode service.  1258 
Table 34 lists the assignment of Master  and Device  to their roles as initiator (I) or receiver  (R)  1259 
in the context of the execution of their individual DL -A services.  1260 
Table 34 – DL-A services within  Master  and Device  1261 
Service name  Master  Device  
OD R I 
PD R I 
EventFlag  I R 
PDInStatus  I R

PDTrig  I

7.3 Data link layer protocol  1393 
7.3.1  Overview  1394 
Figure 28 and Figure 29 are showing the structure of the data link layer and its components;  a 1395 
DL-mode  handler, a message  handler, a Process Data  handler, and an On-request Data  handler 1396 
to provide the specified  services. Subclauses 7.3.2  to 7.3.8  define the behaviour  (dynamics) of 1397 
these handlers by means of UML state machines  and transition tables.  1398 
The On-request  Data  handler supports  three independent types of data: ISDU , command  and 1399 
Event . Therefore , three additional state machines are working together with the On-request  1400 
Data  handler state machine as shown in Figure 30.  1401 
  1402 
Figure 30 – State machines of the data link layer  1403 
Supplementary sequence or activity diagrams are demonstrating certain use cases. See 1404 
IEC/TR  62390 and ISO/IEC  19505.  1405 
The elements each handler is dealing with, such as messages, wake -up procedures, interleave 1406 
mode, ISDU (Indexed Service Data Units), and Events are defined within the context of the 1407 
respective handler.  1408 
7.3.2  DL-mode handler  1409 
7.3.2.1  General  1410 
The Master  DL-mode handler shown in Figure 28 is responsible to setup the SDCI 1411 
communication using services of the Physical Layer (PL) and internal administrative call s to 1412 
control and monitor the message  handler  as well as the states of other handlers.  1413 
The Device  DL-mode handler shown in Figure 29 is responsible to detect a wake -up request 1414 
and to establish communication. It receives MasterCommands to synchronize with the Master 1415 
DL-mode handler states STARTUP, PREOPERATE, and OPER ATE and manages the activation 1416 
and de -activation of handlers as appropriate.   1417 
7.3.2.2  Wake -up procedures and Device  conformity rules  1418 
System Management  triggers the following actions on the data link layer with the help of the 1419 
DL_SetMode service ( requested m ode = STARTUP).  1420

The Master  DL-mode handler tries to establish communication via a wake -up request 1421 
(PL_WakeUp.req) followed by a test message with M-sequence  TYPE_0 ( read " MinCycleTime ") 1422 
according to the sequence shown  in Figure 31. 1423 
  1424 
Figure 31 – Example of an attempt to establish communication  1425 
After the wake -up request (WURQ), specified  in 5.3.3.3 , the DL -mode handler requests  the 1426 
message  handler to send the first test  message after a time TREN (see Table 10) and TDMT (see 1427 
Table 42). The specified  transmission rates of COM1, COM2, and COM3  are used in descending 1428 
order  until a response is obtained, as shown in the example of Figure 31: 1429 
Step : Master  message with transmission rate of COM3 ( see Table 9). 1430 
Step : Master  message with transmission rate of COM2 ( see Table 9). 1431 
Step : Master  message with transmission rate of COM1 ( see Table 9). 1432 
Step : Device response message with transmission rate of COM1.  1433 
Before initiating a (new) message, the DL -mode handler shall wait at least for a time of TDMT. 1434 
TDMT is specified  in Table 42. 1435 
The following conformity rule  appl ies for Device s regarding support of transmission rates:  1436 
• a Device  shall support only one of the transmission rate s of COM1, COM2, or COM3.   1437 
If an attempt to establish communication fail s, the Master  DL-mode handler shall not  start a 1438 
new retry wake -up procedure until after a time TDWU as shown in Figure 32 and specified  in 1439 
Table 42. 1440

initial wake -up retry sequence fails, the Device shall reset its C/Q line to SIO mode after a time 1444 
TDSIO  (TDSIO  is retrigged in the Device after each detected WURQ). The Master shall not trigger 1445 
a new wake -up retry sequence until after a time TSD. 1446

7.3.2.3  Fallback procedure  1458 
System Management  induces  the following actions on the data link layer with the help of the 1459 
DL_SetMode service ( mode = INACTIVE):  1460 
• A MasterC ommand "Fallback" ( see Table B. 2) forces the Device  to change to  the SIO mode . 1461 
• The Dev ice shall accomplish the transition to the SIO mode after 3 MasterCycleTime s and/or 1462 
within maximum TFBD after the MasterCommand "Fallback" . This allows for possible retries 1463 
if the MasterCommand failed indicated through a negative Device response.  1464 
• The Master shall ensure waiting at least maximum TFBD before initiating the next start -up 1465 
procedure.  1466

7.3.3  Message  handler  1510 
7.3.3.1  General  1511 
The role of the message  handler is specified  in 7.1 and 7.2.2.1 . This s ubclause specifies  the 1512 
structure and types of M-sequences  and the behaviour  (dynamics) of the message  handler.  1513 
7.3.3.2  M-sequences  1514 
A Master  and its Device  exchang e data by means of a sequence of message s (M-sequence) . 1515 
An M-sequence  comprises a message from  the Master  followed by a message from  the Device  1516 
as shown in  Figure 38. Each message  consist s of UART frame s. 1517 
All the multi -octet data types shall be transmitted as a big -endian sequence, i.e. the most 1518 
significant octet (MSO) shall be sent first, followed by less significant octets in descending 1519 
order, with the least significant octet (LSO) being sent last, as shown in Figure 2. 1520 
The Master message starts with the "M -sequence Control" (MC) octet, followed by the 1521 
"CHECK/TYPE" (CKT) octet, and optionally followed by either "Process Data" (PD) and/or "On -1522 
request Data" (OD) octets. The Device message in turn starts optionally with "Process Data" 1523 
(PD) octets and/or "On -request Data" (OD) octets, followed by the "CHECK/STAT" (CKS) octet.   1524 

===PAGE 89===
Interface and System  © IO -Link  – 89 – V1.1.5  
 1525 
Figure 38 – SDCI message sequences  1526 
Various M-sequence  types can be selected to meet the particular needs of an actuator or sensor 1527 
(scan rate, amount of Process Data ). The length of Master  and Device  messages  may vary 1528 
depending on the type of  messages  and the data transmission direction, see  Figure 38. 1529 
Figure 39 presents an overview of the defined M-sequence  types. Parts within dotted lines 1530 
depend on the read or write direction within the M-sequence  control octet.  1531

The fixed M -sequence types consist of TYPE_0, TYPE_1_1, TYPE_1_2, and TYPE_2_1 1534 
through TYPE_2_5. Caution: The former TYPE_2_6 is no more supported. The variable M -1535 
sequence types consist of TYPE_1_V and TYPE_2_V.  1536 
The different M -sequence types meet the various requirements of sensors and actuators 1537 
regarding their Process Data width and respective conditions. See A.2 for details of M -sequence 1538 
types. See A.3 for the timing constraints with M -sequences.  1539 
7.3.3.3  MasterCycleTime  constraints  1540 
Within state STARTUP and PREOPERATE a Device is able to communicate  in an acyclic 1541 
manner . In order to detect the disconnecting of Devices it is highly recommended for the Master  1542 
to perform from this point on a periodic communication ("keep -alive message") via acyclic  M-1543 
sequences  through  the data link layer. The minimum recovery  times for acyclic communication 1544 
specified in A.2.6  shall be considered.  1545 
After th ese phase s, c yclic Process Data  communication can be started by the Master  via the 1546 
DL_SetMode  (OPERATE) service. M-sequence  types for the cyclic data exchange shall be used 1547 
in this communication phase to exchange Process Data (PD) and On -request Data with  a Device  1548 
(see Table A. 9 and Table A. 10). 1549 
The Master  shall use for time tCYC the value indicated in the Device parameter  1550 
"MasterCycleTime " (see Table B.1) with a relative tolerance of  -1 % to +10  % (including jitter).  1551 
In cases, where a Device has to be switched back to SIO mode after parameterization, the 1552 
Master  shall send a command "Fallback"  (see Table B. 2), which is  followed by a confirmation 1553 
from the Device.  1554

The message handler takes care of the special communication requirements within the states 1561 
"EstablishCom", "Startup", "PreOperate", and "Operate" of the DL -Mode handler. An internal 1562 
administrative call MH_Conf_COMx in state "Inactive_0" causes the message handler to send 1563 
"test" messages with M -sequence TYPE_0 and different transmission rates of COM3, COM2, 1564 
or COM1 during the establish communication sequence.  1565 
The state "Startup_2" provides all the communication means to support the identity checks of 1566 
System Management  with the help of DL_Read and DL_Write services. The message handler 1567 
waits on the occurrence of these services to send and receive messages (acyclic 1568 
communication).  The state "Preoperate_ 6" is the checkpoint for all On -request Data activities 1569 
such as ISDUs, commands, and Events for parameterization of the Device. The message 1570 
handler waits on the occurrence of the services shown in Figure 40 to send and receive 1571 
messages (acyclic communication). The state "Operate_ 12" is the checkpoint for cyclic Process 1572 
Data exchange. Depending on the M-sequence  type the message handler generates Master 1573 
messages with Process Data acquired from the Process Data handler via the PD service and 1574 
optionally On -request Data acquired from the On-request Data handler  via the OD service .  1575

handler may need several cycles until the ISDU handler returns to the 
"idle" state.

the Event handler to provide the OD service in correspondence to the 
EventFlag service ( see Figure 55, Transition T2). The message handler 
may need several cycles until the Event handler returns to the "idle" state.   
T17 6 7 The Message handler invokes the ODTrig service for the On -request 
handler ( see  Figure 48), which is in state "ISDU_1". In this state it causes 
the ISDU handler to provide the OD service in correspondence to the 
DL_Write service ( see  Figure 51, Transition T13).

7.3.4  Process Data  handler  1600 
7.3.4.1  General  1601 
The transport of output Process Data  is performed using the D L_OutputUpdate services and 1602 
for input Process Data  using the D L_InputTransport services ( see Figure 28). A Process Data  1603 
cycle is completed when the entire  set of Process Data  has been transferred between Master  1604 
and Device  in the requested direction . Such a cycle can last for more than one M-sequence . 1605 

===PAGE 97===
Interface and System  © IO -Link  – 97 – V1.1.5  
All Process Data  are transmitted within one M-sequence  when using  M-sequences  of TYPE _2_x 1606 
(see Figure 39). In this case t he execution time of a Process Data  cycle is equal to the cycle 1607 
time tCYC. 1608 
7.3.4.2  Interleave mode  1609 
All Process Data and On-request Data are transmitted in this case with multiple alternating M-1610 
sequences  TYPE_1_1  (Process Data ) and TYPE_1_2 ( On-request Data ) as shown in Figure 1611 
45. It demon strates the Master  messages writing output Process Data to a Device . The service 1612 
parameter PDOutAddress indicates the partition of the output PD to be transmitted ( see 1613 
7.2.2.3 ). For input Process Data the service parameter PDInAddress correspondingly indicates 1614 
the partition of the input PD. Within a Process Data  cycle all input PD shall be read first followed 1615 
by all output PD to be written. A Process Data  cycle comprises all cycle times required to 1616 
transmit the complete Process Data .  1617 
 1618 
Figure 45 – Interleave mode for the segmented transmission of Process Data 1619 
Interleave mode is for legacy Devices only.  1620

7.3.5  On-request Data handler  1641 
7.3.5.1  General  1642 
The Master On-request Data  handler is a subordinate state machine active in the "Startup_2", 1643 
"PreOperate_3", and "Operate_4" state of the DL -mode handler ( see Figure 35). It controls 1644 
three  other state machines, the so -called ISDU  handler, the co mmand  handler, and the Event  1645 
handler.  It always starts with the ISDU handler by default.  1646 
Whenever an EventFlag.ind is received, the state machine will change to the Event  handler. 1647 
After the complete readout of the Event  information it will return to the ISDU handler state.  1648 
Whenever a DL_Control.req or PDInStatus.ind service is received while in the ISDU handler or 1649 
in the Event  handler, the state machine will change to the command handler. Once the 1650 
command has been served, the state machine will return to the previously active state (ISDU 1651 
or Event ).  1652 
7.3.5.2  State machine of the Master On-request Data handler  1653 
Figure 48 shows the Master  state machine of the On-request Data  handler.   1654 
The On-request Data handler redirects  the ODTrig.ind service primitive for the next message 1655 
content to the currently active subsidiary handler ( ISDU , command, or Event ). This is performed 1656 
through one of the ISDUTrig, CommandTrig, or EventTrig calls.  1657

7.3.6  ISDU  handler  1683 
7.3.6.1  Indexed Service Data Unit (ISDU)  1684 
The general structure of a n ISDU is demon strated in  Figure 50 and specified  in detail in Clause 1685 
A.5. 1686 
 1687 
Figure 50 – Structure of the ISDU  1688 
The sequence of the elements corresponds to the transmission sequence. The elements of an 1689 
ISDU can take various forms depending on the type of I -Service ( see A.5.2  and Table A. 12). 1690 
The ISDU allows accessing data objects (parameters and commands) to be transmitted ( see 1691 
Figure 6). The data objects shall be addressed by the “Index” element.  1692 
All multi -octet data types shall be transmitted as a big-endian  sequence , i.e. the most significant 1693 
octet (MSO) shall be sent first, followed by less significant octets in descending order, with the 1694 
least significant octet (LSO) being sent last, as shown in Figure 2. 1695 
7.3.6.2  Transmission of ISDUs  1696 
An ISDU  is transmitted via the ISDU  communication  channel ( see Figure 8 and A.1.2 ). A number 1697 
of messages  are typically required to perform this transmission (s egmentation) . The Master  1698 
transfers a n ISDU by sending a n I-Service (Read/Write) request to the Device  via the ISDU 1699 
communication  channel. It then receives  the Device ’s response via the same  channel.  1700 
In the ISDU communication  channel , the "Address " element within the M-sequence  control octet 1701 
accommodates a counter (= FlowCTRL) . FlowCTRL is  control ling the segmented data flow  (see  1702 
A.1.2 ) by counting the M-sequences necessary to transmit an  ISDU . 1703 
The receiver of an ISDU expects a FlowCTRL + 1 in the next message in case of undistur bed 1704 
communication. If FlowCTRL is unchanged, the previously transmitted message is repeated. In 1705 
any other case the ISDU structure is violated.   1706 
The Master  uses the "Length" element  of the ISDU and FlowCTRL to check  the accomplishment 1707 
of the complete transmission .  1708 
Permissible values for FlowCTRL are specified  in Table 52. 1709 
I-Service
ExtLength
Index
Subindex
Data 1
...
Length
Data n
CHKPDU = Checksum
I-Service
ExtLength
Index
Subindex
Data 1
...
Length
Data n
CHKPDU = Checksum

===PAGE 103===
Interface and System  © IO -Link  – 103 – V1.1.5  
Table 52 – FlowCTRL definitions  1710 
FlowCTRL  Definition  
0x00  to 0x0F  COUNT  
M-sequence  counter within a n ISDU . Increments beginning with 1 after an ISDU  START. 
Jumps back from 15  to 0 in the Event  of an overflow.  
0x10  START  
Start of a n ISDU  I-Service , i.e., start of a request or a response.  
For the start of a request, any previously incomplete services may be rejected.  
For a start request associated with a response, a Device  shall send “No Service” until its 
application returns response data  (see  Table A. 12). 
0x11  IDLE 1  
No request for ISDU transmission.  
0x12  IDLE 2 : Reserved for future use  
No request for ISDU transmission.  
0x13  to 0x1E  Reserved  
0x1F  ABORT  
Abort entire service.  
The Master  responds by rejecting received response data.  
The Device  responds by rejecting received request data and may generate an abort.  
 1711 
In state Idle_1, values 0x12 to 0x1F shall not lead to a communication error.  1712

7.3.7  Command  handler  1732 
7.3.7.1  General  1733 
The co mmand  handler passes the control code  (PDOUTVALID or PDOUTINVALID)  contained 1734 
in the DL_ Control.req  service  primitive  to the cyclically operating message  handler via the 1735 
OD.req service  and MasterCommands . The message handler uses the page communication 1736 
channel.  1737 
The permissible control codes for output Process Data are listed in Table 55. 1738 
Table 55 – Control codes  1739 
Control code  MasterCommand  Description  
PDOUTVALID  ProcessDataOutput Operate  Output Process Data valid  
PDOUTINVALID  DeviceOperate  Output Process Data invalid or missing  
 1740 
The command handler receives input Process Data status information via the PDInStatus 1741 
service and propagates it within a DL_Control.ind service primitive.  1742 
In addition, the command handler translates Device mode change requests from System 1743 
Management  into corresponding MasterCommands ( see Table B. 2). 1744

7.3.8  Event handler  1768 
7.3.8.1  Events  1769 
There are two types of Event s, one without details, and another one with details. Events without 1770 
details may have been implemented in legacy Devices, but they shall not be used for Devices 1771 
in accordance with this standard. However, all Masters shall support processing of both Event s 1772 
with details and Event s without details.  1773 
The general structure and coding of Event s is specified  in A.6. Event codes without details are 1774 
specified  in Table A. 16. EventC odes with details are specified  in Annex  D. The structure of the 1775 
Event  memory  for EventC odes with details within a Device  is specified  in Table 58. 1776 
Table 58 – Event memory  1777 
Address  Event slot 
number  
Parameter Name  Description  
0x00   StatusCode  Summary of status and error information. Also used to 
control read access for individual messages.  
0x01  1 EventQualifier  1 Type, mode and source of the Event  
0x02  EventCode 1  16-bit EventCode of the Event  
0x03   
0x04  2 EventQualifier  2 Type, mode and source of the Event  
0x05  EventCode 2  16-bit EventCode of the Event  
0x06   
      … 

===PAGE 109===
Interface and System  © IO -Link  – 109 – V1.1.5  
Address  Event slot 
number  
Parameter Name  Description  
0x10  6 EventQualifier  6 Type, mode and source of the Event  
0x11  EventCode 6  16-bit EventCode of the Event  
0x12   
0x13 to 0x1F    Reserved for future use  
 1778 
7.3.8.2  Event processing  1779 
The Device  AL writes an Event  to the Event  memory  and then sets the "Event flag " bit , which 1780 
is sent to the Master  in the next message  with in the CKS octet ( see 7.3.3.2  and A.1.5 ).  1781 
Upon reception of a Device reply message with the "Event flag" bit = 1 , the Master  shall switch 1782 
from the ISDU  handler to the Event  handler. The Event  handler starts reading the StatusC ode.  1783 
If the "Event Detail s" bit is set  (see Figure A. 22), the Master  shall read the Event  details of the 1784 
Event s indicated in the StatusCode from the Event  memory . Once it has read an Event  detail, 1785 
it shall invoke the service DL_E vent .ind . After rec eption of  the service  DL_Event Conf , the 1786 
Master  shall write any data to the StatusCode to reset the "Event flag " bit . The Event  handling 1787 
on the Master  shall be completed regardless of the contents of the Event  data received  1788 
(Event Qualifier, EventC ode).  1789 
If the "Event Details" bit is not set ( see Figure A. 21) the Master Event  handler shall generate 1790 
the standardized Events according to Table A. 16 beginning with the most significant bit in the 1791 
EventCode.  1792 
Write access to the StatusCode indicates the end of Event  processing to the Device . The Device 1793 
shall ignore the data of this Master Write access. The Device  then resets the "Event flag" bit 1794 
and may now change the content of the fields in the Event  memory .  1795

8.3 Application layer protocol  2084 
8.3.1  Overview  2085 
Figure 8 shows that the application layer offers services for data objects which are transformed 2086 
into the special communication  channels of the data link layer.  2087 
The application layer manages the data transfer with all its assigned ports. That means, AL 2088 
service calls need to identify the particular port they are related to.   2089 
8.3.2  On-request Data transfer  2090 
8.3.2.1  OD s tate machine of the  Master  AL 2091

application layer.  2093

Table 74 – State s and transition s for the OD state machine of the Master  AL 2099 
STATE NAME  STATE DESCRIPTION  
OnReq_Idle_0  AL service invocations  from the Master  applications or from the SM Portx handler ( see 
Figure 57) can be accepted within this state.  
Build_DL_Service_1  Within this state AL service calls are checked,  and corresponding DL services are 
created within the subsequent states. In case of an error in the arguments of the AL 
service a negative AL confirmation is created and returned .  
Await_DL_Param_cnf_2  Within this state the  AL service call is transformed in a sequence of as many  
DL_ReadParam or DL_WriteParam  calls as needed (Direct Parameter page access; 
see page communication channel in Figure 7). All asynchronously occurred AL service 
invocations except  AL_Abort are rejected (see 3.3.7 ).  
Await_DL_ ISDU _cnf _3 Within this state the AL service call is transformed in a DL_ISDU Transport service call  
(see ISDU communication  channel in Figure 7). All asynchronously occurred AL service 
invocations except AL_Abort are rejected (see 3.3.7 ).  
Build_AL_cnf_4  Within this state an AL service confirmation is created depending on an argument error, 
the DL service confirmation , or an AL_Abort.

8.3.3.3  Single Event scheduling  2158 
Figure 66 shows how a single Event from a Device is processed, in accordance with the relevant 2159 
state machines.  2160 
• The Device application creates an Event request (Step  1), which is passed from the AL to 2161 
the DL and buffered within the Event  memory (see Table 58).  2162 
• The Device AL activates the EventTrigger service to raise the Event flag, which causes the 2163 
Master to read the Event from the Event memory.  2164

• The Master then propagates this Event to the gateway application (Step  2), and waits for an 2165

8.3.3.4  Multi Event transport  (legacy Devices only)  2173 
Besides the method specified  in 0 in which each single Event  is conveyed through the layers 2174 
and acknowledged by the gateway application, all Master s shall support  a so-called "multi Event  2175 
transport"  which allows up to 6 Events to be transferred  at a time. The Master AL transfers  the 2176 
Event set as a single diagnosis indication to the gateway application and returns a single  2177 
acknowledgment  for the entire set to the legacy Device application.  2178 
Figure 66 also applies for the multi Event  transport, except that this transport uses one 2179 
DL_Event indication for each Event  memory slot , and a single AL_Event indication for the entire 2180 
Event set.  2181 
One AL_ Event.req carries up to 6 Events and one AL_Event.ind indicates up to 6 pending 2182 
Event s. AL_Event.rsp and AL_Event.cnf refer to the indicated entire Event set.  2183

9 System Management  (SM) 2201 
9.1 General  2202 
The SDCI System Management  is responsible for the coordinated startup of the port s within the 2203 
Master  and the corresponding operations within the  connected Device s. The difference between 2204 
the SM of the Master  and the Device  is more significant  than with the other layers.  2205 
Consequently, the structure of this clause separates the services and protocols of Master  and 2206 
Device .  2207 
9.2 System Management  of the Master  2208 
9.2.1  Overview  2209 
The Master  System Management  services are used to set up the Master  ports and the system 2210 
for all possible operational modes . 2211 
The Master  SM adjusts ports through  2212 
• establishing the required communication protocol revision  2213 
• checking the Device  compatibility (actual Device  identifications match expected values)  2214 
• adjusting adequate Master  M-sequence  types and MasterCycleTime s  2215 
For this it uses the following services shown in Figure 69: 2216

9.2.3  SM Master  protocol  2401 
9.2.3.1  Overview  2402 
Due to the comprehensive configuration, parameterization, and operational features of SDCI  2403 
the description of the behavio r with the help of state diagrams becomes rather complex. Similar 2404 
to the DL state machines clause 9.2.3  uses the possibility of submachines within the main state 2405 
machines.  2406 
Comprehensive compatibility check methods are performed w ithin the submachine states . 2407 
These methods are indicated by "do method " fields within the state graphs, for example in  2408 
Figure  72.  2409 
The corresponding decision logic is demonstrated via activity diagrams ( see Figure 73, Figure 2410 
74, Figure 75, and Figure 78).  2411 
9.2.3.2  SM Master  state machine  2412 
Figure 71 shows the main state machine of the System Mangement Master . 2413 
Two submachines for the compatibility and serial number check are specified in subsequent 2414 
sections.  2415 
In case of communication disruption the System Management is informed via the service 2416 
DL_Mode (COMLOST).  2417 
Only the SM_SetPortConfig service allows reconfiguration of a port.  2418 
The service SM_Operate causes no effect in any state except in state "wait_4".  2419 
  2420

Some states contain complex logic to deal with the compatibility and validity checks. Figure 73 2438 
to Figure 76 are demonstrating the context.  2439 
Figure 73 shows the decision logic for the protocol revi sion check in state "CheckVxy ". In case 2440 
of configured Device s the following rule applies: if the configured revision (CR ID) and the real 2441 
revision (RR ID) do not match, the CRID  will be transmitted to the Device . If the Device  does 2442 
not accept, the Master  returns an indication via the SM_Port Mode  service with REV _FAULT.  2443 
In case of not configured Device s the operational mode AUTO COM shall be used.  See 9.2.2.2  2444 
and 9.2.2.3  for the parameter name abbreviations.   2445

9.2.3.5  Rules for the usage of M-sequence  types  2472 
The System Management  is responsible for setting up the correct M-sequence  types. This 2473 
occurs after the check compatibility actions (transition to PREOPERATE) and before the 2474 
transition to OPERATE.  2475 
Different M-sequence  types shall be used within the different operational states  (see A.2.6 ). 2476 
For example, when switching to the OPERATE state the M-sequence  type relevant for cyclic 2477 
operation shall be used. The M-sequence  type to be used in operational state OPERATE is 2478 
determined by the size  of the input and output Process Data . The available M-sequence types 2479 
in the three modes STARTUP, PREOPERATE, and OPERATE and the corresponding coding of 2480 
the parameter M-sequenceCapability are  specified  in A.2.6 . The input and output data formats 2481 
shall be acquired  from the connected Device  in order to adjust the M-sequence  type . It is 2482 
mandatory for a Master  to implement a ll the specified  M-sequence  types  in A.2.6 . 2483

Interface and System  © IO -Link  – 145 – V1.1.5  
 2488 
Figure 79 – Structure and services of the System Management  (Device ) 2489 
The System Management  (SM) o f the Device  provides  the central controlling instance via the 2490 
Line Handler through  all the phases of initialization, default state (SIO),  communication startup , 2491 
communication, and fall back to SIO mode.  2492 
The Device  SM interacts with the PL to establish the necessary line driver and receiver 2493 
adjustments ( see Figure 16), with the DL to get the necessary information from the Master  2494 
(wake -up, transmission rates, a.o.) and with the Device  applications to ensure the Device  2495 
identity and compatibility  (communication and identification  parameters) .  2496 
The transitions between the line handler states ( see Figure 81) are initiated by the Master  port 2497 
activities (wake -up and communication)  and triggered through the Device  Data Link Layer via 2498 
the DL _Mode indications and DL _Write requests (commands).  2499 
The SM provides the Device  communication and identification  parameters through the Device  2500 
applications interface . 2501 
The sequence chart in Figure 80 demonstrates a typical Device  sequence from initialization to 2502 
default SIO mode and via wake -up request from the Master  to final communication. The 2503 
sequence chart is complemented by the use case of a communication error such as TDSIO  2504 
expired, or communication fault, or a request from Master  such as Fallback (caused by Event ). 2505

SM_SIO_1  In SM_SIO the SM Line Handler  is remaini ng in the default SIO m ode. The P hysical 
Layer is set to the SIO m ode characteristics defined by the Device  application via  the 
SetDeviceMode service. The state is left on receiving a DL_Mode( ESTABCOM ) 
indication.  
SM_ComEstablish_2  In SM_ComEstablish the SM is waiting for the communication to be established in the 
Data Link Layer. The state is left on receiving a DL_Mode(INACTIVE)  or a 
DL_Mode(COMx) indication, where COMx may be any of COM1, COM2 or COM3.  
SM_ComStartup_3  In SM_ComStartup the communication parameter (D irect Parameter page 1, addresses 
0x02  to 0x06 ) are read by the Master SM via DL_Read requests. The state is left upon 
rece ption of  a DL_Mode(INACTIVE), a DL_Mode(OPERATE) indication ( legacy Master 
only) , or a DL_Write( MCmd_ MASTERIDENT) request (Master in accordance with this 
standard ). 
SM_IdentStartup_4  In SM_IdentStartup the identification data (VID, DID, FID) are read and verified by the 
Master . In case of incompatibilities the Master  SM writes the supported SDCI Revision 
(RID) and configured Device ID (DID) to the Device . The state is left upon rece ption of  a 
DL_Mode(INACTIVE), a DL_Mode(PREOPERATE) indicati on (compatibility check 
passed) , or a DL_Write( MCmd_ DEVICEIDENT) request (new compatibility requested).  
SM_IdentCheck_5  In SM_IdentCheck the SM waits for new initialization of communication and 
identification parameters. The state is left on receiving a DL_Mode(INACTIVE) 
indication , a DL_Read( Direct Parameter page 1, addresses 0x02  = "MinCycleTime" ) 
request , or the SM requires a switch of the transmission rate.   
Within this state the Device  application shall check the RID and DID parameters from 
the SM and set these data to the supported values. Therefore the following sequence 
of services shall be executed between Device  application and SM.  
Invoke SM_GetDeviceCom(configured RID, parameter list)  
Invoke SM_GetDeviceIdent(configured DID, parameter list)  
Invoke  Device  application checks and provides compatibility function and parameters  
Invoke SM_SetDeviceCom(new supported RID, new parameter list)  
Invoke SM_SetDeviceIdent(new supported DID, parameter list)  
SM_CompStartup_6  In SM_CompatStartup the communication and identification data are reread and 
verified by the Master  SM. The state is left on receiving a DL_Mode(INACTIVE) or a 
DL_Mode(PREOPERATE) indication . 
SM_Preoperate_7  During SM_Preoperate the SerialNumber  can be read and verified by the Master  SM, 
as well as Data Storage and Device  paramet erization may be executed. The state is 
left on receiving a DL_Mode(INACTIVE), a DL_Mode(STARTUP)  or a 
DL_Mode(OPERATE) indication.  
SM_Operate_8  During SM_Operate the cyclic  Process Data  exchange and acyclic On-request Data  
transfer are active. The state is left on receiving a DL_Mode(INACTIVE) or a 
DL_Mode(STARTUP) indication.

10 Device  2739 
10.1  Overview  2740 
Figure 85 provides an overview of the complete structure and services of a Device . 2741 
 2742 
Figure 85 – Structure and services of a Device  2743 
The Device  applications comprise first the technology specific application  consisting of the 2744 
transducer with its technology parameters, its diagnosis information, and its Process Data . The 2745 
common Device  applications comprise : 2746 
• Parameter Manager  (PM), dealing with compatibility and correctness checking of complete 2747 
sets of technology (vendor) specific and common system parameters (see  10.3 ); 2748 
• Data Storage  (DS)  mechanism, which optionally uploads or downloads parameters to the 2749 
Master  (see 10.4 ); 2750 
• Event Dispatcher  (ED), supervising states and conveying diagnosi s information such as 2751 
notifications, warnings, errors,  and Device  requests  as peripheral initiat ives (see 10.5 ); 2752 
• Process Data Exchange (PDE) unit, conditioning the data structures for transmission in case 2753 
of a sensor or preparing the received data structures for signal generation . It also controls 2754 
the operational states to ensure the validity of Process Data  (see 10.2 ). 2755 
These Device  applications provide standard methods /functions  and parameters common to all 2756 
Device s, and Device  specific functions and parameters , all specified within Clause  10. 2757 
10.2  Process Data Exchange (PDE)  2758 
The Process Data Exchange unit cyclically transmits and receives Process Data  without 2759 
interference from the On-request Data  (parameters, commands, and Event s). 2760

An actuator (output Process Data) shall observe the cyclic transmission and enter a default 2761 
appropriate state, for example keep last value, stop, or de-energize, when ever  the data 2762 
transmission is  interrupted  (see 7.3.3.5  and 10.8.3 ). The actuator shall wait on the 2763 
MasterCommand  "ProcessDataOutputOperate"  (see Table B. 2, output Process Data "valid" ) 2764 
prior to  regular operation after restart in case of an interruption . 2765 
Within cyclic  data exchange , a n actuator (output Process Data) receive s a Master -Command  2766 
"DeviceOperate", whenever the output Process Data are invalid and a Master -Command 2767 
"ProcessDataOutputOperate" , whenever they become valid again ( see Table B. 2). 2768 
There is no need for a  sensor Device  (input Process Data)  to monitor  the cyclic data e xchange . 2769 
However, if  the Device  is not able to  guarantee  valid Process Data , the PD status " Process 2770 
Data invalid"  (see A.1.5 ) shall be signaled  to the Master  application.  2771 
10.3  Parameter  Manager  (PM) 2772 
10.3.1  General  2773 
A Device  can be parameterized via two basic methods  using the Direct Parameters or the Index 2774 
memory space accessible with the help of ISDU s ( see Figure 6).  2775 
Mandatory for all Device s are the so -called Direct Parameters  in page 1 . This  page 1 contains 2776 
common communication and identification parameters  (see B.1).  2777 
Direct Parameter page 2 optionally offers space for a maximum of 16 octets of technology 2778 
(vendor ) specific parameters for Device s requiring not more tha n this limited number  and with 2779 
small system footprint ( ISDU  communication not implemented, easier fieldbus handling  possible  2780 
but with less comfort ). Access to the Direct Parameter page 2 is performed via A L_Read and 2781 
AL_Write ( see 10.8.5 ). 2782 
The transmission of parameters to and from the spacious Index memory can be performed in 2783 
two ways: single parameter by single parameter or as a block of parameters. Single parameter 2784 
transmission as specified in 10.3.4  is secured via several checks and confirmation of the 2785 
transmitted parameter. A negative acknowledgment  contains an appropriate error description 2786 
and the parameter is not activated. Block Parameter  transmission as specified in 10.3.5  defers 2787 
parameter consistency checking and activation until after the complete transmission. The 2788 
Device performs the checks upon reception of a special command and returns a confirmation 2789 
or a negative acknowledgment  with an appropriate error description. In this case the transmitted 2790 
parameters shall be rejected and a roll back to the previous parameter set shall be performed 2791 
to ensure pro per functionality of the Device . 2792 
10.3.2  Parameter manager  state machine  2793 
The Device  can be parameterized  using ISDU  mech anisms  whenever the P M is active. The 2794 
main functions of the PM are the transmission of parameters to the Master  ("Upload"), to the 2795 
Device  ("Download"), and the consistency and validity checking within the Device  2796 
("ValidityCheck") as demonstrated in Figure 86. 2797 
The P M is driven by command messages of the Master  (see Table B. 9). For example,  the guard 2798 
[UploadStart] corresponds to the reception of the SystemCommand  "ParamUploadStart" and 2799 
[UploadEnd] to the reception of the SystemCommand  "ParamUploadEnd".  2800 
NOTE  1 Following  a communication interruption, the Master  System Management  uses the service 2801 
SM_Device Mode with the variable " INACTIVE " to stop the upload process and to return to the "IDLE" state.   2802 
Any new "ParamUploadStart" or "ParamDownloadStart" while another sequence is pending, for 2803 
example due to an unexpected shut -down of a vendor parameterization tool, will abort the 2804 
pending sequence. The corresponding parameter changes will be discarded.  2805 
NOTE  2 A PLC user program  and a parameterization tool can conflict (multiple access) , f or example  if during 2806 
commissioning , the user did not disable accesses from the PLC program while  changing parameters via the tool.  2807 
The parameter manager  mechanism in a  Device  is always activ e and the DS_ ParUpload.req in 2808 
transition T4 is used to trigger the Data Storage (DS) mechanism in 10.4.2 . 2809 

===PAGE 160===
V1.1.5  – 160 – Interface and System  © IO -Link
